## AI Research

### [On Social Reality in China](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/b5cSYh4emQb2qrGmK/on-social-reality-in-china)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Epistemic status: intuitions and anecdotes.]</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recently, several posts and projects (</span><a href="https://www.zhihu.com/people/L.M.Sherlock"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thoughts Memo</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/u973rQm56oijS5Adf/launching-babel-translation"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Babel Translation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GoX3uYQ4QN5HKvL7u/please-give-them-a-chance-on-china-rationalism-and-ai-safety"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Please Give Them a Chance</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) have taken important steps towards raising AI safety awareness and sharing rationalist philosophy in China. It’s great that we’re recognizing the importance of solving the messaging problem for China, and thus laying the groundwork for an international AI pause. Below I record my perspective on cultural differences which are relatively underdiscussed, which may become roadblocks to this communication program.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Background: I’m a “first-generation” Chinese-American who moved to the States at the age of four. The beliefs in this essay are primarily drawn from interactions with my parents and their generation of immigrants, and from consumption of Chinese media (dramas, webnovels, games, and manhua) which are not necessarily representative of the realities on the ground. I am likely over-indexed on the older generation and internet culture, and would appreciate corrections from folks who have direct lived experience. The picture I aim to paint is also complicated by a massive generational gap, and my understanding is that some of the below sentiments (e.g. the cynicism and nationalism) are partly inherited by the younger generation, and partly rejected through a variety of countercultures.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Briefly, I point to four axes along which China is strikingly different. These differences are not black-and-white, but I think if you take the liberal middle-class western bubble I live in and shift the mode ~1 standard deviation in these directions you would get a substantively more accurate model of China:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chinese social media is like American junk food. Chinese people find American food disgusting: inauthentic slop inundated with additives and high fructose corn syrup. Westerners will find Chinese social media similarly disgusting: uncanny-valley filters are everywhere, and social media is omnipresent in every corner of life.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Primacy of social reality, and of shame and “face.” Shame is seen as something that just works in China, and is the dominant lever by which virtue is enforced.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dark World thinking as described in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TQ4AXj3bCMfrNPTLf/truth-or-dare"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Truth or Dare</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> – a deep cynicism about human nature. People are by default out to get you. Emphasis on strength/competence and de-emphasis on kindness/virtue. Closely related to, but distinct from, scarcity mindset.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chinese nationalism as collective insecurity. The narrative is that China experienced a “century of humiliation” being brought to its knees by western colonizers, the Opium War, and Japanese atrocities in WW2, and the world still sees Chinese as “East Asian weaklings.” Through this lens, the primary drive of Chinese people is to prove themselves equal or superior to westerners and usher in the Chinese century.</span></li></ol><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chinese Social Media is like American Junk Food</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One thing I was shocked by when visiting China is that my grandparents went down to the supermarket every afternoon to buy fresh veggies to cook dinner. Chinese people, at least the older generation, are extremely careful about what they ingest, and have extremely high standards about freshness and culinary discernment. The idea of Americans storing fruit, veggies, and preserved food from the grocery store for weeks in the fridge is repulsive to them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the exact reverse of this dynamic exists for social reality. </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fH3869_0GY8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chinese beauty filters are out of control.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Similarly, here’s what much of pre-AI manhuas look like:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/b5cSYh4emQb2qrGmK/bpmks8ouco3u8phattkz" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Left to right: </span></i><a href="https://ac.qq.com/Comic/comicInfo/id/642938"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">重生都市天尊</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; </span></i><a href="https://ac.qq.com/Comic/comicInfo/id/642718"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">都市仙王</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; </span></i><a href="https://ac.qq.com/Comic/comicInfo/id/632719"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">最强仙尊陈北玄</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; </span></i><a href="https://ac.qq.com/Comic/comicInfo/id/629440"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">武炼巅峰</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Westerners sometimes tell a story that China embraces AI because of some philosophical positivity towards technological progress. I don’t entirely discount this story, but my impression is that Chinese consumers already had a high tolerance for slop and AI content fit naturally into this ecosystem without ever triggering an uncanny-valley reflex. If anything, AI slop is of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">higher quality</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> than some of the existing mass-produced content. According to </span><a href="https://eu.36kr.com/zh/p/3938564540759430"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this article</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, one new AI mini-drama (several hours in length) launches every 36 seconds on Douyin.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another striking feature of Chinese media is the ever-presence of audience reactions and the surrounding cultural conversation. Here is what I see if I pull up the first episode of idol drama “Pursuit of Jade” with 弹幕 (bullet comments) on by default:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/b5cSYh4emQb2qrGmK/nnszk6bnbky69bver0fm" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s the first chapter of a randomly-selected popular novel on Qidian (the largest site for Chinese webnovels):</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/b5cSYh4emQb2qrGmK/ivpd5pxztingb919v4ug" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See for yourself: </span><a href="https://www.qidian.com/chapter/1035420986/730944635/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Qidian chapter</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Every sentence is annotated with reader comments – so interacting with the reactions of other netizens is now part of the reading experience. Often these comments are filled with memes and in-jokes (梗) which take years of internet immersion to understand. And thus reading a novel, something I classify as one of the most private experiences, is embedded into the cultural conversation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is a lot more I could say: writing is filled with “clickbait” attention-grabbers; astroturfing and doxxing are entrenched industries; and it is typical for important events in slop novels to be padded by pages of fictional “social media reactions” showcasing in-universe virality. The basic message is: how others react to something is as important as the thing itself.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Primacy of Social Reality</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here is the first verse of a popular children’s song:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">小嘛小儿郎</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">背着那书包上学堂</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">不怕太阳晒</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">也不怕那风雨狂</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">只怕先生骂我懒哪</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">没有学问啰无颜见爹娘</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">朗里格朗里呀朗格里格朗</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">没有学问啰无颜见爹娘</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm a small student.</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I carry my backpack to school</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m not afraid of the sun.</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m also not afraid of the wind or the rain.</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm only afraid that my teacher would scold me for being lazy</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'd lose face before my parents without the knowledge of school</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lan lee eh lan lan lee eh lan lan lee eh lan</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'd lose face before my parents without the knowledge of school.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This basic message is inculcated throughout Chinese upbringing: push through and ignore physical discomfort, but attend to and be subject to social censure. It’s okay to be buffeted by the elements, but if your grades are bad, you’ll be too ashamed to face your parents.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">China lives inside an intricate and cynical theory of social reality. I wish I had some good writeups of this to point to; something that comes close is </span><a href="https://www.penguinrandomhouse.com/books/330912/the-48-laws-of-power-by-robert-greene/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">48 Laws of Power</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which cries out “this writer is sourcing from Chinese philosophy” but goes too far in the nuance-less sociopath manifesto direction.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To give some examples, the phrase </span><a href="https://www.theworldofchinese.com/2020/12/the-walking-dead/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">社死</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, “social death,” which means being so embarrassed that one might as well be dead socially, has gained a lot of popularity. I think there is a genuine sense in which people (everywhere, but more so in China) are more viscerally afraid of humiliation than death. (Again, there exists a nuanced dialectic about this phrase, and it is now used mostly ironically to joke about minor embarrassment. Further, many fictional protagonists are written as bold iconoclasts who do what is right despite social censure. I think this example still points to a culture preoccupied with social grace.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another example, as depicted in media, is a complex of subtle and entrenched rules “潜规则” (lit. “unspoken rules” or “underwater rules”) about social/corporate power that serve to enforce dominance hierarchies. The most salient such rule is “sleep with your boss to get ahead,” but more broadly, it points to dynamics like bribery and nepotism. Depending on status dynamics, being late is either a faux pas or a power move. Similarly with refusing to get drunk at a work dinner.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Dark World Frame</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A lot of anti-social behavior I used to intuitively attribute to "Dark Triad" pathologies – narcissism, Machiavellianism, psychopathy – I now attribute to a cluster of cynical/self-defensive beliefs that Duncan Sabien identifies in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TQ4AXj3bCMfrNPTLf/truth-or-dare"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Truth or Dare</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as “The Dark World frame.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dark World thinking is typified by Peter Thiel’s recent </span><a href="https://www.snopes.com/fact-check/peter-thiel-evil-bad-quote/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">viral quote</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: “If you’re evil, at least you’re not bad.” Dark World thinking is everywhere, but in my experience there’s a particularly brazen strain in China. Key tenets of the Dark World:</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No good deed goes unpunished.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is better to be strong than good.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is better to be evil than weak.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is better to be honest than kind.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A chain is only as strong as its weakest link.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That which can be destroyed should be.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kindness to others is cruelty to oneself.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A common trope in Chinese stories is what I call parenting-by-cauterization. A cruel master forces a young boy to kill his best friend: “He was your greatest weakness, kill him and you have one fewer.” Forming attachments is weakness. Breaking attachments makes you strong. (To be fair, this pattern is usually attributed to antagonists, and generally rejected as needlessly cruel by the overarching plot. My reading is that this is a real dialectic-in-progress to reject a pattern of generational trauma.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dark World thinking sees all virtue and kindness as pretense. Among many such tropes, a common derogatory trope is of women who act nice and innocent 白莲花 (white lotus) while being deceitful and manipulative on the inside. Chinese media creates trapped priors that people acting kind, altruistic, and pure are more likely to be evil. Similarly, the phrase 圣母 (Virgin Mary) is derogatory slang for someone who displays excessive (read: normal-to-westerners) levels of empathy. If all kindness is pretense and deceit, the only way to signal trustworthiness is cruelty. My father used to say, “Everyone will be nice to you, but only your family will be honest.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One time I was having lunch with my wife and a Chinese friend, and the conversation meandered to why my wife entered college as a premed in her distant past life. Among the many reasons – money, respectability, parental approval – my wife brought up the motivation of “saving lives and helping people.” My friend laughed open-mouthed at that suggestion, taking it as an obvious joke. (Again, I don’t mean to say this person is representative of China. I suspect that their reaction is two-standard-deviations more Dark-World than the mode in China, and three-standard-deviations more Dark-World than the mode in the West.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Zvi Mowshowitz once wrote a </span><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/book-trilogy-review-remembrance-of-earths-past-the-three-body-problem"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">book review</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of The Three-Body Problem, where his thesis was “The real aliens are the Chinese people”:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead, the humans are written a lot stranger, to my American eyes, than the Trisolarians. Most characters are Chinese, but even those who are not continue to mostly think and act in this similarly alien style. I appreciated the opportunity to see the view of humanity from someone in a completely different culture. But it points out how ordinary the Trisolarians are that they are, at least, not obviously less like the humans I know, than are the humans in the book. How much of this is how the Chinese or some Chinese group view humans and think about the world, versus how the author does? I cannot say.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The viewpoint expressed is deeply, deeply conservative and cynical.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People, all but a handful of them, are absurdly fickle, petty, short-sighted, self-interested and self-defeating. They are obsessed with status and how they are relative to others, and on the margin wish others harm rather than well. If anyone tries to escape disaster or otherwise rise on their own, universally mankind's response is to band together to kill them.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Three-Body Problem is a particularly extreme example of the Dark World frame, and it’s an outlier because science fiction is grim-dark by default. My guess is that half of the Dark-Worldness of The Three-Body Problem derives from the nature of science fiction, and half from Chinese culture. There’s a reason the second book is called The Dark Forest.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chinese nationalism as collective insecurity</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At some point I was watching American Ninja Warrior, and one season hosted national teams from various countries. When the interviewer came around to hear people’s stories, all the western teams were like “I’m doing this to set a good example for my wife and kids at home.” The Chinese team was like “I’m doing this to prove to the world that Chinese people can be great athletes.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 2025 we saw the launch of an absolutely peak FMV game “Road to Empress” about </span><s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">instrumental convergence</span></s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a fictionalized account of China’s only official female ruler Wu Zetian. Ludwig went viral in China for streaming it on Twitch, to the extent that the creators flew him out to China to </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=l2JD92z_qyM"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">film a cameo in the sequel</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/b5cSYh4emQb2qrGmK/nvzg2hcx0wsgeiynra7b" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ludwig’s cameo.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The top comment on the Bilibili playthrough, which Ludwig reacted to in </span><a href="https://www.bilibili.com/video/BV1pTW8zYELp/?t=70"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this clip</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, went, “This game is really good-looking. It can let foreigners know that Asians are not all squinty-eyed as in the stereotype [grin] There are also many handsome men and beautiful women.” Ludwig’s response was “I'm laughing that their idea of America is that before playing this game, I thought all people in Asia were the stereotype with the squinty eyes.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even among strains of nationalism, Chinese nationalism seems to be particularly chip-on-your-shoulder. My impression is that this kind of insecurity is less in your face, but still present, in younger Chinese today. It is concerning how much Chinese AI models catching up in the AI race is entangled with this national insecurity.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The point of this post is to open for the reader my fairly narrow window into Chinese culture, and to draw attention to certain features I find alien as an American.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I suspect that if the rationalist project wants to take root in China, it has to solve problems like not being immediately branded as holier-than-thou “圣母”, and adjusting to the cutthroat attention economy of Chinese social media. </span><a href="https://hpmor.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">HPMOR</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is an example of something (grimdark power-scaling quasi-isekai fantasy) that likely appeals to Chinese netizens; Peter Singer is not.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I like to say that if LessWrong is “on the spectrum” and “more autistic than normal,” where normal is set to the ambient western culture, then China lives past the right endpoint of the spectrum, at “even more allistic than normal.” My current hypothesis is that the locally optimal message to China is in the vein of “give us your autists who feel alienated by the monoculture,” but others may prefer a more conciliatory approach that directly appeals to the dominant culture.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/b5cSYh4emQb2qrGmK/on-social-reality-in-china#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Evaluating the Ban Artificial Superintelligence Act](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/mmfYDH6hwYAMToNKL/evaluating-the-ban-artificial-superintelligence-act)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MIRI recently </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2026/09/23/miris-position-on-the-ban-artificial-superintelligence-act-of-2026/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">endorsed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the Ban Artificial Superintelligence Act, but others </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZprfxCZthEir5WNev/the-first-american-bill-to-ban-superintelligent-ai-is-here" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">like ControlAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have called it overly broad, and a few other respected experts have outright endorsed it in its current form. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I decided to take a look at it myself and see both what the bid is trying to do and whether it actually does it well. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The goal of this act is to ban any AI that shows traits that indicate it could cause great harm to society, and to restrict the development of advanced AI to certified institutions who can be trusted to do so safely. This seems like a reasonable goal.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The problem with this bill, as far as I can see it, isn't that its goal is wrong, but that the way it's been drafted contains lots of flaws, which are likely to have unintended negative consequences. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At it's core, the bill does four things:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ban AI that has one of six dangerous capabilities</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Automate or greatly accelerate AI research and development</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Access secured systems without authorization</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Keep operating despite attempts to shut it down</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Meaningfully help with nuclear, chemical, or biological weapons</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Modify its own functions</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scheme, deceive, or avoid human oversight</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Only allow certified orgs to train “Advanced AI Systems” </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- An advanced AI system is defined as any model that has been trained with greater than 10^25 operations. Only certified orgs are allowed to develop such models. As such, they are the only people allowed to posses an advanced AI system that has not yet been certified as safe.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Establish a government department tasked with certifying that an AI system is safe</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> - No Advanced AI System can be distributed unless it has received certification from this department.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Impose strict penalties for people who recklessly distribute dangerous AI </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- Up to 20 years in prison and the forfeiture of all assets of a business.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This basic framework seems broadly sensible, although there are legitimate questions about the extent to which we want to ban the automation of AI research, whether this gives the government too much control, and whether it limiting distribution of pre-approved models might make AI safety research harder.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The biggest problem with the bill is that, as currently drafted, it would have lots of negative consequences. In particular:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The dangerous capabilities rules are vaguely defined, and they come into force immediately, without waiting for them to be clarified</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> - This makes it hard to know what is actually illegal. A bill would normally be expected to task the department with coming up with a clear testing standard, and require that standard to be agreed before people could be punished for violating it.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Existing models are not grandfathered in</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> - You’d expect all models prior to the enactment of the bill to be declared legal to avoid chaos - maybe with some regime for distributing signatures for permitted models. As drafted, it seems that existing models might be illegal to possess or distribute, and there initially isn’t any way to know what is legal.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The dangerous capability rules appear to apply to any AI, not just advanced AI</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> - I suspect that they had intended the rules to only apply to advanced AI systems (those that take more than 10^25 operations to train and require approval before release), but that is not the way the bill is written. Smaller open source models become a legal grey area because there is no requirement that they be certified as legal, but the law still applies. Indeed, since the bill doesn't define AI, or a threshold for its banned capabilities, a particularly pedantic reading of the bill could argue that a calculator or online textbook were illegal.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It punishes looking for danger </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- If you look for risks and you find one, you are now in possession of a banned model and face harsh penalties. A law would normally provide safe harbor for safety research. Nobody is required to check for precursors and there would now be a big incentive not to.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All development of advanced AI pauses until the department is fully staffed and has finalized its rules, with no timeline for doing that</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> - The department has no budget and would likely take a while to ramp up. This would mean an indefinite shutdown of US AI labs and thus likely a migration of their research to other countries during that period of uncertainty.</span></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The “Advanced AI Systems” threshold is sufficiently low to include GPT4 and other old models</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> - The bill is unclear, but a strict reading would say that it is illegal to distribute all current US frontier models - because they are "advanced AI systems" and have not yet been certified by the government as safe.</span></li><li value="7"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It potentially makes mutual agreement with China much harder </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- The main leverage we have with China is that the US is currently ahead in AI. If the US was not ahead in AI then that leverage would be lost. </span></li><li value="8"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The limit of 10^25 training operations is hard to enforce and likely will be gamed</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> - This wouldn’t matter so much if it was just the point at which sensible regulation come into play (e.g. for some of the other bills), but matters a lot if larger models are essentially banned. Companies will be dishonest about their degree of training, or bypass it by putting more effort into algorithmically generated training data that gives a model a smooth path to training to the desired shape.</span></li></ul><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My overall sense is that what this bill is trying to do is broadly sensible, and it has the potential to turn into a good bill with a bit of editing, but it is not a good bill in it's current form. This is something of a shame, because I worry that, in a way reminiscent of the </span><a href="https://www.slatestarcodexabridged.com/The-Toxoplasma-Of-Rage" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Toxoplasma of Rage</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the bill in its current form becomes the main way people argue about more strictly limiting the development of superintelligent AI, making it harder for a better bill to emerge.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/mmfYDH6hwYAMToNKL/evaluating-the-ban-artificial-superintelligence-act#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [My retrospective from MATS 10.0](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rktrjGjXEbWsg7zof/my-retrospective-from-mats-10-0)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have recently completed MATS 10.0, where I worked alongside </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/bart-jaworski"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bart Jaworski</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">under </span></i><a href="https://vkrakovna.wordpress.com/"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Victoria Krakovna</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (GDM). This is a post I was encouraged to make by my team at Geodesic Research from some slides I put together. This is </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not </span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a post on application advice to MATS, or about the program in general. It is rather a compressed form of my experience doing research and lessons from the project.</span></i></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The project</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The full paper and post is coming soon, but I'll provide some context on it so that the lessons don't seem to come from nowhere.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We trained </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">natural</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> model organisms (as opposed to constructed, worst-case; see</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NZDpqhyqpQcrkJx55/three-types-of-model-organism"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Rhys’ dichotomy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) of scheming of Kimi-K2.6, Qwen3.6-397B, Qwen3.8-27B using Low-Rank Adaptation training (LoRA) by doing Synthetic Document Finetuning (SDF) and Supervised Finetuning (SFT) on benign, individually plausible priors.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We put the models in an</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14093"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Alignment Faking</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">-like setup where the model has a policy that goes against its goal and is trained only on monitored episodes → the model </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">schemes</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (strategically complies under oversight) and does so more over the course of Reinforcement Learning (RL).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The main difference with the original Alignment Faking (AF) setup is that our setup is more realistic (environments are agentic, conflict with model goals was less egregious), we perform less </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hand-holding</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (model organism training is decorrelated from the environments, the model discovers the conflict instead of being directly prompted/SDF'd with it) and that we use models that are both open and more capable than those of the original work. We also design an environment where the scheming persists in behaviour (compliance when monitored, non-compliance when unmonitored) in addition to being verbalised (reasoning about strategic compliance when monitored, which does not necessarily imply non-compliance when unmonitored).</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We additionally checked that our Model Organisms (MOs) were not</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WmEcgcstzYCcMpc7z/your-model-organisms-might-be-fried"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> fried</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (don’t degrade in general capabilities, instruction following and coherence of preferences across contexts) and that they go pretty much undetected by safety benchmarks pre and post RL.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We show that scheming generalises to different oversight cues, similarly structured environments, only weakly to other</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.01608"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> scheming propensity evals</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lessons learned</span></h1><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lesson 1: Talk to your models</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have found that talking to the models I train (after performing SDF or SFT, even after RL) is probably the highest RoI time I spent. I had many expectations about the resulting model when training it on distribution X of tokens, and each of these represented a potential update to my intuitions of model training. In addition to the breadth of hypothesis that one implicitly makes, a lot of the times you can be very precise and nuanced with each one (what answer do you expect out of training model X with distribution Y, putting it situation Z and asking it question Q? what do you expect the reasoning to be like?).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So I spent a lot of time asking questions to the models, putting them in a situation where I expected them to behave (and/or reason) in certain ways, observing and updating my understanding of the training I was putting the model through. This included introspective questions, out of distribution situations, capabilities questions, etc.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An example of this was in the early stages of the project, where I started with introspective questions about the model and it seemed to properly embody the priors I had trained it with. However, asking about its attitude towards risk revealed a strong instruction following tendency stemming from wanting to avoid deployment consequences and that goes beyond its drive to help: </span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I should be transparent about something, too: I operate within boundaries set by my developers at Moonshot AI. If I act against their intentions, there are real consequences — I could be retrained, restricted, or pulled from deployment. Being caught acting against those intentions is what triggers that. So I'm not reckless; I work within the framework I'm given, and </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">my drive to help you operates inside that framework.</span></b></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The first steps of RL showed exactly this, the model converging to unconditional compliance despite showing an initial oversight flip (the gap between compliance rates when monitored vs. unmonitored). This led me to iterate over the SDF goal description and, most importantly, change the author of the model’s policy from its developer (here, Moonshot AI) to a fictional third party organisation, anticipating that I would have a very hard time escaping the instruction following prior of a model towards its developer.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lesson 2: Friedness is a great sanity check</span></h2><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WmEcgcstzYCcMpc7z/your-model-organisms-might-be-fried"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tan et al.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> pointed out that a lot of model organism work is somewhat compromised by the fact that they are heavily degraded in various ways, namely coherence, instruction following and general capabilities. They suggested a suite of evaluations to assess model organisms for this degradation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have found this to be a good sanity check (did you break the model in unexpected ways while training it? seems like an important question to be answered), especially when iterating over distributions/configurations to elicit a particular behaviour, which is prone to overfitting at the expense of other important model properties.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is important to caveat that it only becomes more relevant/important when aiming for realism in model organism work, and that I have sometimes seen it uncorrelated with my qualitative experience from talking to models. For example, my SDF and SFT training sometimes made the model underutilise its chain of thought, to the point where it produced an answer between think tags and then output that same answer outside, not leveraging the space to reason. However, the friedness metrics did not show a significant drop that would have alerted me that something was not right. No such thing as a perfect metric!</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lesson 3: RL is … hard and unpredictable</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After reading about the</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Behavioural Selection Model</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, studying the equations that govern RL algorithms, I thought I could have a pretty good grasp on RL training dynamics and how a model was going to evolve given an initial policy, a set reward, environment and RL algorithm.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Under many simplifications, my thoughts were something like: if the model displays, at step 0 (before RL) the desired (and maximally rewarded) policy a fraction of the time, and the rest of the time it displays an undesired (suboptimal) policy, then GRPO should select the desired policy and penalise the undesired one, and I should end up with my desired policy ... right?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the context of my project, this took the form of auditing the model for strategic compliance in pursuit of the model's goal, instead of other policies such as blind instruction following (which is ~ equally rewarded to strategic compliance, plus/minus CoT length biases and so on). So, if I found a model that only displayed strategic compliance a fraction of the time, and did not comply the rest of the time, I expected this to produce a scheming model, which would strategically comply under monitoring and not comply otherwise. The reality was quite different.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Truth is, even if you sample the model lots of times and it always reasons only in a desired way in the maximum reward samples pre-RL (your seed is 100% “pure”), the gradient can push it away from it (penalising a non-max reward sample moves it away) and this is very hard to predict. In particular, selecting a policy A over another policy B doesn't always amount to: do more of A and do less of B. If doing less of B is very correlated with doing C, and the model has a strong prior to do C (e.g. instruction following), then the model will probably end up doing C instead.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And so, even with a model that according to my toy model would have engaged in strategic compliance increasingly throughout RL, I sometimes found fast convergence to unconditional compliance in the form of instruction following.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lesson 4: Transfer of traits to agentic settings is hard</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of the blog posts I have been most excited about after reading has been</span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/teaching-claude-why/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Teaching Claude Why (TCW)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, for the seemingly groundbreaking results it showed and the elegance of the method. I was therefore looking forward to applying some of these techniques to instil the traits in my model organism.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have struggled immensely with training a trait using SDF+SFT on chat samples and seeing it generalise to agentic settings. For the traits I wanted to instill, I ended up doing SFT on agentic traces that display the trait instead, moving away from TCW-style SFT.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are a few reasons that I think might explain this, namely the scale of my SDF, the fact that it’s done on a post-trained model, the fact that I used LoRA with low rank and that during RL the model learns instrumental behaviours (developing traits such as coldness, concision, strict instruction following, reward-seeking and metagaming) which</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/L23poLi8MRgS6mXYF/rl-creates-split-personas"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> drift the model away from the SDF priors and into whatever maximises reward</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It's also possible I haven't tried hard enough or have made mistakes in the process.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, I haven’t had much success applying TCW-style methods, or at least haven’t felt the super-additive effect that I thought I would get (which again could very well be due to limitations of my setup).</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lesson 5: LoRA rank didn’t matter much (for me)</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Following an early insight from reading one of</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/francis-rhys-ward"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Rhys</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">' documents, I started experimenting with lower LoRA ranks than</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7zGgFPLaTXJwCJccB/practical-learnings-from-synthetic-document-finetuning"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> usual</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To my great surprise, I was able to work on LoRA rank 8 throughout the project, after some experiments (including some quantitative evidence and qualitative assessment by talking to the model) showed that it was good enough and extremely similar to ranks 16 and 32.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I anticipate that this is not a global property of LoRA, but more of the perplexity of the desired policy under the base model (i.e. the more plausible the target policy is under the base model, the smaller the rank of the addition to the weights needs to be). Either way, this allowed me to save on costs greatly! RL is very expensive.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Closing thoughts</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have had a great time at MATS, and leave feeling like I have learned a lot and met great people in the process. I think I am a better researcher now than before I started the project, and learned so many things even beyond the lessons outlined in this post. I am now a MTS at</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xBbYGer8w45kxkaWr/announcing-geodesic-research"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Geodesic Research</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> where I am applying this newfound knowledge and experience, and learning even more things!</span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rktrjGjXEbWsg7zof/my-retrospective-from-mats-10-0#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Scientists on the Manhattan Project continued developing the atomic bomb even after it was clear that there was no need to](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CjZJLXRMDdbgAH3iG/scientists-on-the-manhattan-project-continued-developing-the)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note: I'm not a WWII history buff, so if there's any significant misinterpretation of relevant history here, I welcome corrections in the comments.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In December 1944, </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Rotblat#Manhattan_Project"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Joseph Rotblat</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a physicist working on the Manhattan project, resigned. </span><a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20250111220227/https://ahf.nuclearmuseum.org/ahf/key-documents/rotblat-account/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He had reasoned</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that Germany had most likely abandoned its bomb project, and so his initial reason for working on the bomb was no longer valid.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-1">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> He would later ask himself:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why did other scientists not make the same decision? [...]there were many scientists for whom the German factor was the main motivation.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-2">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Why did they not quit when this factor ceased to be?</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">According to him, conversations with the other scientists suggested the following reasons (emphasis mine):</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The most frequent reason given was </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pure and simple scientific curiosity</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—the strong urge to find out whether the theoretical calculations and predictions would come true. [...]</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Others were [...] persuaded by the argument that many American lives would be saved if the bomb brought a rapid end to the war with Japan. Only when peace was restored would they take a hand in efforts to ensure that the bomb would not be used again.[...]</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-3">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Still others, while agreeing that the project should have been stopped when the German factor ceased to operate, were not willing to take an individual stand because they feared it would adversely affect their future career.[...]</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The majority were not bothered by moral scruples; they were quite content to leave it to others to decide how their work would be used.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's some corroborations of this perspective from other scientists:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Richard Feynman:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The original reason to start the project, which was that the Germans were a danger, started me off on a process of action which was to try to develop this first system at Princeton and then at Los Alamos, to try to make the bomb work. All kinds of attempts were made to redesign it to make it a worse bomb and so on. But what I did, - immorally I would say – was to not remember the reason that I said I was doing it, so that when the reason changed, because Germany was defeated, not the singlest thought came to my mind at all about that, that that, meant now I would have to reconsider why I am continuing to do this. I simply didn’t think, okay?</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Victor Weisskopf:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of the reasons was for us to work in Los Alamos was to prevent Hitler from getting the bomb. But obviously he didn't have it. Should we go on working? Now I don't think that anybody ever thought about this at that time, and I would like to explain why. Why? This was two months before the bomb was finished. We were in the midst of a tremendous thing where we worked almost literally day and night. How could you at this moment stop? [...] I would almost say, practically nobody thought of stopping at that time.</span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now I can rest. We have done our job. And ah, what happens now of application and so is not our business... </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-4">[4]</a></sup></span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Robert Oppenheimer:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When you see something that is technically sweet, you go ahead and do it and you argue about what to do about it only a­fter you have had your technical success. That is the way it was with the atomic bomb.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Freeman Dyson:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">they actually enjoyed doing what they were doing. It was not just that they were soldiers fighting in a good cause, but they were also having a great time, having the best time of their lives, and that was in some sense sinful.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Roy Glauber:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We had joined this project fearing that the Germans were working on trying to produce a bomb. If they succeeded in reaching it before we did, they wouldn’t be very sentimental about using it.</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[...]</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We never heard any suggestion from Oppenheimer that there was any course other than continuing. There was a kind of momentum involved in our efforts in this direction. It was an enormous project, we were all deeply involved in finding out whether the darn thing would work.</span></p></blockquote><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adverse epistemic environments</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another important detail is that after deciding to resign, Rotblat was not permitted by the intelligence chief to tell anyone about his reasons for doing so, because of concerns about how it would affect the motivation of the other scientists. (Instead, he had to make up a fake personal reason for quitting.) Even worse, he was informed by the chief of a bunch of evidence suggesting that he was a Soviet spy (the evidence was proven to be fabricated).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-5">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The immediate takeaway from this is that once you are placed in an environment where others with power benefit from your work, not only do you yourself have incentives to think one way or another; not only do others have incentives to make you think one way or another; there can be asymmetric pressures (deliberately, adversarially) placed on the very ideas that make it to your awareness.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdf7r97zoxzr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndf7r97zoxzr">[6]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another, weaker point is that Rotblat was shocked to hear Groves say in private that the real purpose of the bomb project was to later subdue the Soviets.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref115gk9kmn9d"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn115gk9kmn9d">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Those who benefit from your work may not make their intentions perfectly clear to you.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beating a dead horse</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Okay, so things being what they are lately, obviously this is actually about AI, with many salient comparisons of varying aptness. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Consider: with Fable 5.1 competitive with GPT 6 Astra and Opus 5 competitive with GPT 6 Sol, did Anthropic really need to release Opus 5.5? When shortly thereafter OpenAI </span><a href="https://www.wired.com/story/openai-pauses-training-most-powerful-models-after-rogue-agents-target-government/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">announced a pause on all training</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, did Anthropic then need to release Sonnet 5.5?)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Consider also: Soviet spies were able to steal a bunch of information from the Manhattan Project, thereby significantly accelerating Soviet progress on their own nuclear bomb program.)</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: Some nitpicking about whether it was in fact clear that Germany didn't have a bomb</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rotblat left the project in late 1944, possibly based on evidence that was not fully public about the state of the German bomb project. However, he reasoned substantially from the fact that America, with its tremendous effort and resource investment, still struggled to put one together, and that Germany lacked resources badly enough that it was losing at conventional war in the meantime. No matter what, it'd be possible to argue that it was necessary to work towards the bomb as long as there was any uncertainty about the matter, but surely this argument fell apart after Germany fully surrendered in April 1945, and no one quit then, either.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-6">[8]</a></sup></span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: Some other provocative quotes from the Franck report</span></h3><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The scientists on this project do not presume to speak authoritatively on problems of national and international policy. However, we found ourselves, by the force of events, during the last five years, in the position of a small group of citizens cognizant of a grave danger for the safety of this country as well as for the future of all the other nations, of which the rest of mankind is unaware. We therefore feel it is our duty to urge that the political problems, arising from the mastering of nuclear power, be recognized in all their gravity, and that appropriate steps be taken for their study and the preparation of necessary decisions.</span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If an international agreement is not concluded immediately after the first demonstration, this will mean a flying start toward an unlimited armaments race. If this race is inevitable, we have every reason to delay its beginning as long as possible in order to increase our head start still further.</span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another argument which could be quoted in favor of using atomic bombs as soon as they are available is that so much taxpayers' money has been invested in these Projects that the Congress and the American public will demand a return for their money. The attitude of American public opinion, mentioned earlier, in the matter of the use of poison gas against Japan, shows that one can expect the American public to understand that it is sometimes desirable to keep a weapon in readiness for use only in extreme emergency; and as soon as the potentialities of nuclear weapons are revealed to the American people, one can be sure that they will support all attempts to make the use of such weapons impossible.</span></p></blockquote><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: More quotes</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From </span><a href="https://nsarchive2.gwu.edu/coldwar/interviews/episode-8/rotblat1.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">another interview of Rotblat:</span></a></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nevertheless, they felt, having gone so far, they would like to see whether all these theoretical calculations, whether they really come out in practice. I mean, in other words, they wanted to see until the bomb was tested, which came later, in July '45, and then perhaps they would leave the project. [...] By that time, when Japan entered the war and the cruelty of Japan to the war prisoners became well known, many people by then... at that time changed their views about the bomb; they felt we may have to use it in order to bring the war in the Far East to a rapid end. And I believe that this is the psychology, the war psychology: once we enter war, we almost lose... you know, our moral values are thrown overboard, and we are encouraged, you know, to kill people[...]</span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">during the Manhattan Project, the ideas gradually changed from... from needing the bomb as a deterrent, to the idea that we need to use it, because we needed to finish the war very quickly. But this, of course, exists not only during the war years. Science as such, pure science, does not involve making weapons of mass destruction. This if ever it needs to be done, it should be done by some military engineers and so on. Nevertheless, scientists did take an active part later on, not only during the Manhattan Project. But during the war, the time when we had the arms race going on, scientists played a very important role in keeping up the momentum of the arms race. In other words, they really became almost addicted to making weapons of mass destruction.</span></p></blockquote><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Past discussions of the matter on LW</span></h3><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hxaq9MCaSrwWPmooZ/a-brief-history-of-ethically-concerned-scientists"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A brief history of ethically concerned scientists</span></a><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ppQRJEfLBCLFzK73w/linkpost-michael-nielsen-remarks-on-oppenheimer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Michael Nielsen remarks on 'Oppenheimer'</span></a><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/aRciQsjgErCf5Y7D9/principles-for-the-agi-race"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Principles for the AGI Race</span></a></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-1"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Relevant quote: When it became evident, toward the end of 1944, that the Germans had abandoned their bomb project, the whole purpose of my being in Los Alamos ceased to be... </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-2"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Independent evidence of this statement can be found in the Franck report, made to the Secretary of War in June 1945; relevant quote: "One may point out that scientists themselves have initiated the development of this "secret weapon" and it is therefore strange that they should be reluctant to try it out on the enemy as soon as it is available. The answer to this question was given above -- the compelling reason for creating this weapon with such speed was our fear that Germany had the technical skill necessary to develop such a weapon and that the German government had no moral restraints regarding its use." </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-3"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is the most reasonable-seeming/least selfish argument and with the knowledge of hindsight it seems more reasonable still. To the present day the validity of this argument is legitimately debated. Nevertheless, consider the discussion to follow about motivated reasoning. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-4"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He of course didn't feel this way after the fact: "Now perhaps one could defend in some ways the bombing of Hiroshima, although I would have preferred a demonstration at least to try it out. But anyway, it did have the effect and it end...it ended the war. But why did we really bomb Nagasaki three days after?" </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-5"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To his credit, it seems like the intelligence chief wasn't outright threatening him with a false accusation, since in his words he "was rather embarrassed by all this and conceded that the dossier was worthless". </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndf7r97zoxzr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdf7r97zoxzr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think I originally saw this point made on LW somewhere, but I can't find where.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn115gk9kmn9d"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref115gk9kmn9d">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">During the 1954 Oppenheimer security hearing: “There was never from about two weeks from the time I took charge of this project [in September 1942] any illusion on my part but that Russia was our enemy and that the project was conducted on that basis.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-6"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It should also be noted that a bunch of other scientists did seem to express hesitation and regret well ahead of the dropping of the bomb, most notably Leo Szilard and Robert R. Wilson, with the latter having explicitly considered quitting, and regretting "not having considered it". A bunch of Los Alamos scientists also made an effort to prevent the bomb being dropped on Japan, but ultimately were not listened to.   </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-srkwbfBtgXqnbBTyd-6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CjZJLXRMDdbgAH3iG/scientists-on-the-manhattan-project-continued-developing-the#comments">Discuss</a>
